<a id='question-059'></a>

### Question 059: Renal Pathology: Immune Complex Mediated Glomerulonephritis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Immune complex deposition in the glomeruli leading to complement activation, inflammation, and glomerular damage, specifically testing the recognition of histological features and underlying pathophysiology of post-infectious glomerulonephritis. (免疫复合物在肾小球内的沉积导致补体激活、炎症和肾小球损伤，具体测试后感染性肾小球炎的组织学特征和潜在病理生理机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 7-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with complaints of facial puffiness and dark-colored urine that started 2 days ago. His mother reports that he had a sore throat approximately 2 weeks prior to the onset of these symptoms, which resolved spontaneously. On examination, the boy is hypertensive (110/75 mmHg) and has 2+ pitting edema in both lower extremities. Urinalysis reveals hematuria (RBCs >50/HPF) and proteinuria (3+). Serum creatinine is elevated at 1.8 mg/dL (reference range: 0.5-1.0 mg/dL), and C3 complement level is significantly decreased (reference range: 85-170 mg/dL). A renal biopsy is performed, showing diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis with subepithelial "humps" on electron microscopy. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals granular deposits of IgG and C3 along the glomerular basement membrane and capillary walls.

Which of the following pathological mechanisms is most likely responsible for the patient's renal injury?
(A) Deposition of circulating immune complexes formed by antibodies against glomerular basement membrane antigens.
(B) Formation of in situ immune complexes due to antibodies binding to planted antigens within the glomerulus.
(C) Activation of the alternative complement pathway leading to direct glomerular injury.
(D) Deposition of circulating immune complexes formed by antibodies against antigens from a recent infection.
(E) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity targeting podocytes, leading to proteinuria.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 7-year-old boy. Age is relevant as post-infectious glomerulonephritis (PIGN) is common in children.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Facial puffiness (edema), dark urine (hematuria), onset 2 days ago. Suggests acute kidney injury and glomerular inflammation.
- **Past History:** Sore throat 2 weeks prior. Classic preceding infection for PIGN, often Group A Streptococcus (GAS).
- **Physical Exam:** Hypertension, lower extremity edema. Signs of fluid overload and kidney dysfunction.
- **Urinalysis:** Hematuria (>50 RBCs/HPF), proteinuria (3+). Indicates glomerular damage.
- **Serum Labs:** Elevated creatinine (1.8 mg/dL), decreased C3 complement. Suggests acute kidney injury and complement consumption, characteristic of immune complex-mediated disease.
- **Renal Biopsy:**
    - Light Microscopy: Diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis (hypercellular glomeruli).
    - Electron Microscopy: Subepithelial "humps" (electron-dense deposits). Pathognomonic for PIGN.
    - Immunofluorescence: Granular IgG and C3 deposits along GBM and capillary walls. Indicates immune complex deposition.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the pathological mechanism responsible for renal injury.

The constellation of findings (recent sore throat, acute nephritic syndrome presentation, low C3, subepithelial humps, granular immune deposits) strongly points towards post-infectious glomerulonephritis (PIGN), typically triggered by a preceding infection, most commonly GAS pharyngitis or impetigo. The underlying mechanism involves the formation of circulating immune complexes (antigen-antibody complexes) against antigens from the recent infection (e.g., streptococcal antigens). These complexes circulate in the blood and deposit in the glomeruli, activating the complement system (primarily the classical pathway, explaining low C3) and triggering an inflammatory response that damages the glomeruli.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Infection:** The preceding sore throat suggests a Group A Streptococcus (GAS) infection.
- **Antigen Release:** GAS infection leads to the release of bacterial antigens (e.g., streptococcal pyrogenic exotoxins, enolase, DNA).
- **Antibody Production:** The host immune system produces antibodies (primarily IgG) against these antigens.
- **Immune Complex Formation:** Circulating antigens combine with antibodies to form immune complexes in the bloodstream.
- **Glomerular Deposition:** These circulating immune complexes deposit in the glomerular capillaries, particularly in the subepithelial space.
- **Complement Activation:** The deposited immune complexes activate the classical complement pathway (C1q, C4, C2, C3, C5, C6, C7, C8, C9). This leads to the consumption of complement components, particularly C3, explaining the low serum C3 level.
- **Inflammation:** Complement activation generates anaphylatoxins (C3a, C5a) that recruit inflammatory cells (neutrophils, monocytes) to the glomeruli.
- **Glomerular Injury:** Inflammatory cells release proteases, reactive oxygen species, and cytokines, causing damage to the glomerular basement membrane, podocytes, and mesangial cells. This results in hypercellularity (proliferation of endothelial and mesangial cells, infiltration of leukocytes), hematuria, proteinuria, hypertension, and edema.
- **Histopathology:** Light microscopy shows diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis. Electron microscopy shows characteristic subepithelial "humps" representing the deposited immune complexes. Immunofluorescence shows granular deposits of IgG and C3 along the capillary loops and GBM.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deposition of circulating immune complexes formed by antibodies against glomerular basement membrane antigens. This describes anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome), which typically presents with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and pulmonary hemorrhage. The immune deposits are linear along the GBM on immunofluorescence, not granular. C3 levels are usually normal. This mechanism does not fit the granular deposits, low C3, or subepithelial humps seen in the vignette.
- (B): Formation of in situ immune complexes due to antibodies binding to planted antigens within the glomerulus. This mechanism is characteristic of lupus nephritis (specifically Class V membranous lupus nephritis or Class III focal lupus nephritis), where autoantibodies react with antigens already present in the glomerulus (e.g., nuclear antigens). While immune complexes are involved, they are formed *in situ*, not circulating. The clinical presentation and specific biopsy findings (subepithelial humps) are more typical of PIGN.
- (C): Activation of the alternative complement pathway leading to direct glomerular injury. Activation of the alternative pathway can occur in conditions like C3 glomerulopathy or dense deposit disease (membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis type II). However, PIGN is primarily mediated by the classical pathway due to immune complex deposition. Furthermore, C3 glomerulopathy typically shows dominant C3 deposition on immunofluorescence, often without significant IgG, and lacks the characteristic subepithelial humps.
- (E): T-cell mediated cytotoxicity targeting podocytes, leading to proteinuria. This mechanism is implicated in minimal change disease, the most common cause of nephrotic syndrome in children. Minimal change disease typically presents with heavy proteinuria, hypoalbuminemia, and edema, but without significant hematuria, hypertension, or renal insufficiency. Biopsy shows effacement of podocyte foot processes on electron microscopy, with normal light microscopy and immunofluorescence. This does not fit the nephritic presentation, low C3, or biopsy findings in the vignette.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Post-Infectious GN (PIGN) | Anti-GBM Disease | Lupus Nephritis (Class V) | C3 Glomerulopathy | Minimal Change Disease |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------ | :--------------- | :------------------------ | :---------------- | :--------------------- |
| **Trigger**             | Recent Infection (GAS)    | Autoantibodies  | Autoantibodies (SLE)     | Dysregulation Alt. Pathway | Unknown                |
| **Immune Deposit**      | Circulating ICs           | Anti-GBM Ab     | In situ ICs               | C3               | None                  |
| **Deposit Location**    | Subepithelial (humps)     | Linear GBM      | Subepithelial/Mesangial   | Dense Deposits   | None                  |
| **IF Pattern**          | Granular IgG, C3          | Linear IgG      | Granular IgG, C3, +/- IgA | Granular C3      | Negative               |
| **Complement (C3)**     | Low                      | Normal          | Variable (often normal)   | Low              | Normal                |
| **Clinical Syndrome**   | Nephritic (hematuria, edema, HTN) | RPGN, +/- Pulmonary Hemorrhage | Variable (Nephritic/Nephrotic) | Nephritic/Nephrotic | Nephrotic              |
| **Typical Age**         | Children                 | Young/Middle Age | Young Adults             | Variable         | Children               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Post-infectious glomerulonephritis (PIGN) is a classic example of immune complex-mediated glomerular injury following an infection. The key features are recent infection, acute nephritic syndrome, low C3 complement, and characteristic subepithelial "humps" on electron microscopy with granular IgG and C3 deposits on immunofluorescence. Differentiating PIGN from other glomerulonephritides requires careful attention to the clinical context, complement levels, and specific immunofluorescence patterns (e.g., linear deposits in anti-GBM disease, dominant C3 in C3 glomerulopathy).

<a id='question-060'></a>

### Question 060: Renal Pathology: Immune Complex Deposition
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology of immune complex-mediated glomerulonephritis, specifically membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) type I, focusing on the role of complement activation and the resulting glomerular injury. (考察免疫复合物介导的肾小球肾炎的病理生理学，特别是I型膜性增生性肾小球肾炎，重点是补体激活的作用以及由此产生的肾小球损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with worsening lower extremity edema, fatigue, and dark urine over the past week. He has a history of chronic hepatitis C infection, diagnosed 5 years ago, which he has not treated. His vital signs show a blood pressure of 160/100 mmHg. Physical examination reveals significant pitting edema bilaterally up to the knees and mild periorbital edema. Urinalysis shows 3+ proteinuria, microscopic hematuria (50 RBCs/hpf), and red blood cell casts. Serum creatinine is elevated at 2.5 mg/dL (normal range: 0.6-1.2 mg/dL), and serum albumin is low at 2.8 g/dL (normal range: 3.5-5.0 g/dL). A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows diffuse mesangial hypercellularity and endocapillary proliferation. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals granular deposits of IgG and C3 along the glomerular capillary walls and in the mesangium. Electron microscopy shows subendothelial and mesangial electron-dense deposits, along with thickening of the glomerular basement membrane (GBM) with a "tram-track" appearance.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism leading to the observed glomerular injury in this patient?

(A) Deposition of circulating immune complexes containing antibodies against glomerular basement membrane antigens, leading to linear IgG deposition on immunofluorescence.
(B) Formation of subepithelial immune deposits due to in situ immune complex formation in the presence of anti-GBM antibodies.
(C) Activation of the alternative complement pathway due to dysregulation, leading to C3 deposition without significant immunoglobulin deposition.
(D) Deposition of circulating immune complexes containing hepatitis C viral antigens and associated antibodies, leading to complement activation and glomerular damage.
(E) Formation of immune complexes primarily within the glomerulus, triggered by deposition of antibodies against planted antigens, resulting in mesangial proliferation and IgA deposition.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male with chronic hepatitis C. This is a crucial clue linking the renal disease to a systemic infection known to cause immune complex deposition.
- **Symptoms:** Edema, fatigue, dark urine (hematuria), hypertension. These are classic signs of nephritic syndrome with nephrotic features (proteinuria, edema).
- **Labs:** Proteinuria (3+), hematuria (50 RBCs/hpf), RBC casts (indicative of glomerular inflammation/damage), elevated creatinine (renal failure), low albumin (nephrotic syndrome). This confirms a glomerulonephritis with both nephritic and nephrotic components.
- **Biopsy Findings:**
    - **Light Microscopy:** Mesangial hypercellularity and endocapillary proliferation. This pattern is characteristic of MPGN.
    - **Immunofluorescence:** Granular IgG and C3 deposits along capillary walls and in the mesangium. This indicates immune complex deposition. The granular pattern rules out anti-GBM disease (which is linear).
    - **Electron Microscopy:** Subendothelial and mesangial electron-dense deposits, GBM thickening ("tram-track" appearance). This is the hallmark ultrastructural finding of MPGN type I.
- **Connecting Clues:** The patient has chronic hepatitis C, a known cause of systemic immune complex formation. The biopsy findings (granular IgG/C3, subendothelial/mesangial deposits, tram-tracking) are classic for MPGN type I. MPGN type I is often caused by circulating immune complexes. Therefore, the most likely mechanism is the deposition of immune complexes related to the hepatitis C infection.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Hepatitis C and Immune Complexes:** Chronic hepatitis C infection leads to the production of antibodies against viral antigens (e.g., HCV core protein, E2 envelope protein). These antibodies can form circulating immune complexes with viral antigens.
- **Immune Complex Deposition:** These circulating immune complexes can deposit in various tissues, including the glomeruli.
- **Complement Activation:** Once deposited in the glomeruli, these immune complexes activate the classical complement pathway (due to the presence of IgG).
- **Inflammation and Injury:** Complement activation leads to the generation of anaphylatoxins (C3a, C5a) and the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC, C5b-9). This recruits inflammatory cells (neutrophils, macrophages), causes endothelial cell injury, mesangial cell proliferation, and ultimately leads to glomerular damage, manifesting as MPGN type I.
- **MPGN Type I Pathogenesis:** Characterized by subendothelial and mesangial immune complex deposition, leading to complement activation (especially C3), endothelial cell injury, mesangial cell proliferation, and GBM duplication/thickening ("tram-track" appearance).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deposition of circulating immune complexes containing antibodies against glomerular basement membrane antigens, leading to linear IgG deposition on immunofluorescence. This describes Anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome). The immunofluorescence pattern is linear, not granular, and the deposits are typically along the GBM, not subendothelial/mesangial. This is incorrect.
- (B): Formation of subepithelial immune deposits due to in situ immune complex formation in the presence of anti-GBM antibodies. This is contradictory. Anti-GBM antibodies cause linear deposition along the GBM, not subepithelial deposits. Subepithelial deposits are characteristic of membranous nephropathy, which involves in situ complex formation or deposition of pre-formed complexes (e.g., in lupus nephritis). This is incorrect.
- (C): Activation of the alternative complement pathway due to dysregulation, leading to C3 deposition without significant immunoglobulin deposition. This describes C3 glomerulopathy (C3G), which includes diseases like dense deposit disease (DDD) and C3 glomerulonephritis (C3GN). While C3G involves complement dysregulation and C3 deposition, it typically shows *minimal or no* significant immunoglobulin deposition on immunofluorescence, unlike the granular IgG and C3 seen in this case. This is incorrect.
- (E): Formation of immune complexes primarily within the glomerulus, triggered by deposition of antibodies against planted antigens, resulting in mesangial proliferation and IgA deposition. This describes IgA nephropathy (Berger's disease). The characteristic finding is dominant IgA deposition in the mesangium, not granular IgG and C3 along capillary walls and in the mesangium, nor the typical EM findings of MPGN type I. This is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | MPGN Type I (Correct Answer) | Anti-GBM Disease | Membranous Nephropathy | C3 Glomerulopathy | IgA Nephropathy |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------- | :--------------- | :--------------------- | :---------------- | :------------- |
| **Etiology**            | Circulating Immune Complexes (e.g., HCV, SLE) | Anti-GBM Antibodies | Anti-PLA2R Ab (most common), Circulating Complexes | Complement Dysregulation | IgA Deposition |
| **ImmunoFluorescence**  | Granular IgG, C3 (capillary loops, mesangium) | Linear IgG (GBM) | Granular IgG, C3 (subepithelial) | Granular C3 (variable Ig) | Granular IgA (mesangium) |
| **Electron Microscopy** | Subendothelial, Mesangial deposits; Tram-track GBM | GBM thickening, no deposits | Subepithelial deposits; Spikes | Dense deposits (DDD), Amorphous deposits (C3GN) | Mesangial deposits |
| **Clinical Presentation** | Nephritic/Nephrotic syndrome | Rapidly progressive GN | Nephrotic syndrome | Nephritic/Nephrotic syndrome | Nephritic syndrome (often post-infection) |
| **Associated Conditions** | HCV, SLE, Cryoglobulinemia | Goodpasture's, Lung hemorrhage | Hepatitis B, SLE, Malignancy | Genetic mutations (e.g., Factor H) | Infections, IgA deficiency |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Chronic infections like hepatitis C can lead to the formation of circulating immune complexes that deposit in the glomeruli, causing immune complex-mediated glomerulonephritis, often manifesting as MPGN type I. Key features include granular IgG and C3 deposition on immunofluorescence and subendothelial/mesangial deposits with GBM thickening ("tram-tracking") on electron microscopy. Differentiating MPGN from other glomerulonephritides relies heavily on the pattern and location of immune deposits and the clinical context.

<a id='question-061'></a>

### Question 061: Renal Pathology: Focus 02-11 (Diseases may be divided into those originating in the glomerulus, tubules, interstitium, or vessels)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology and histological features of membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN), specifically Type II (Dense Deposit Disease), and its association with complement dysregulation. (理解膜性增生性肾小球肾炎 (MPGN) 的病理生理学和组织学特征，特别是 II 型 (致密沉积病)，及其与补体功能失调的关系。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with complaints of worsening lower extremity edema, dark urine (cola-colored), and fatigue over the past week. He has a history of recurrent upper respiratory infections since childhood. His past medical history is otherwise unremarkable, and he denies any recent infections or new medications. Vital signs show a blood pressure of 160/100 mmHg. Physical examination reveals significant bilateral lower extremity edema and periorbital edema. Urinalysis reveals 3+ proteinuria, microscopic hematuria (50 RBCs/hpf), and red blood cell casts. Serum creatinine is elevated at 2.5 mg/dL (reference range: 0.6-1.2 mg/dL). A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows diffuse mesangial hypercellularity and endocapillary proliferation. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals diffuse granular deposition of C3 along the glomerular basement membrane (GBM) and in the mesangium. Electron microscopy shows numerous subendothelial and mesangial electron-dense deposits, giving the GBM a "tram-track" appearance. C3 levels in the serum are low. C4 levels are normal.

Which of the following molecular targets is most likely deficient in this patient, leading to the observed renal pathology?

(A) Factor H
(B) C1 inhibitor
(C) C5 convertase
(D) Membrane attack complex (MAC)
(E) Factor B

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male with acute nephritic syndrome symptoms (edema, dark urine, hypertension, fatigue) and nephrotic range proteinuria (3+). This points towards a glomerular disease.
- **History:** Recurrent upper respiratory infections suggest a possible underlying immune dysregulation.
- **Urinalysis:** Proteinuria, hematuria, and RBC casts confirm glomerular inflammation and damage (nephritic syndrome).
- **Labs:** Elevated creatinine indicates acute kidney injury. Low C3 with normal C4 is a crucial clue pointing towards alternative pathway complement activation.
- **Renal Biopsy Findings:**
    - **Light Microscopy:** Mesangial hypercellularity and endocapillary proliferation are characteristic of MPGN.
    - **Immunofluorescence:** Diffuse granular C3 deposition is typical for MPGN, especially Type II.
    - **Electron Microscopy:** Subendothelial and mesangial electron-dense deposits ("tram-track" appearance) are pathognomonic for MPGN Type II (Dense Deposit Disease).
- **Diagnosis:** The clinical presentation, low C3/normal C4, and biopsy findings (especially EM) strongly suggest MPGN Type II (Dense Deposit Disease).
- **Pathophysiology:** MPGN Type II (DDD) is caused by uncontrolled activation of the alternative complement pathway on the glomerular cell surface. This leads to the formation of C3 convertase (C3bBb) and C5 convertase (C3bBbC3b), resulting in excessive C3 cleavage and deposition of C3bBbC3b complexes (dense deposits) within the GBM and mesangium.
- **Molecular Target:** The most common cause of DDD is an autoantibody against C3 convertase (C3bBb), which stabilizes the enzyme and prevents its degradation. Alternatively, genetic mutations or autoantibodies can target regulatory proteins of the alternative pathway, such as Factor H. Factor H normally binds to C3bBb, inactivating it and promoting its dissociation. Deficiency or dysfunction of Factor H leads to persistent C3bBb formation and complement activation. Given the low C3 and normal C4, alternative pathway dysregulation is highly suspected. Factor H deficiency or autoantibodies against Factor H are common causes of DDD.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Complement System Overview:** The complement system is part of the innate immune system. It can be activated via three pathways: classical, lectin, and alternative.
- **Alternative Pathway:** This pathway is continuously active at a low level and is amplified by microbial surfaces or damaged host cells. It involves C3 hydrolysis by C3 convertase (C3bBb). Factor B binds C3b, and Factor D cleaves Factor B to form the active C3 convertase C3bBb. Factor H is a key regulator that binds C3bBb, promoting its dissociation and preventing excessive C3 activation. Properdin stabilizes C3bBb.
- **MPGN Type II (DDD):** This disease results from persistent activation of the alternative pathway, leading to the formation of C3 convertase (C3bBb) on glomerular cells. This convertase cleaves C3 into C3a and C3b. C3b binds to the GBM and mesangium, forming the dense deposits seen on EM. The C3 convertase itself (C3bBb) can also become incorporated into these deposits.
- **Causes of DDD:**
    - **Autoantibodies against C3 convertase (C3bBb):** Stabilize the enzyme, preventing its degradation.
    - **Genetic mutations in complement regulatory genes:** Most commonly *CFH* (Factor H), *CFI* (Factor I), *CD46* (MCP), *C3* (C3 nephritic factor). These mutations lead to decreased function or expression of the regulatory proteins.
    - **Autoantibodies against complement regulatory proteins:** Most commonly Factor H.
- **Low C3/Normal C4:** This pattern is characteristic of alternative pathway activation, as the classical and lectin pathways involve C1, C4, and C2. In DDD, the alternative pathway is dysregulated, consuming C3 but leaving C4 levels relatively normal.
- **Molecular Target in this Case:** The question asks for the *most likely deficient* molecular target. Given the classic presentation of DDD (low C3, normal C4, tram-track deposits), dysregulation of the alternative pathway is the underlying mechanism. Factor H is the most commonly implicated regulatory protein in DDD, either through genetic mutations or autoantibodies. Therefore, Factor H deficiency (functional or quantitative) is the most likely molecular target.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Factor H:** This is the correct answer. Factor H is a key regulator of the alternative complement pathway. Its deficiency (due to mutations or autoantibodies) leads to uncontrolled C3 convertase activity, C3 consumption (low serum C3), and deposition of C3bBbC3b complexes in the glomeruli, characteristic of MPGN Type II (DDD). This fits perfectly with the clinical and pathological findings.
- (B) **C1 inhibitor:** C1 inhibitor (C1-INH) is a regulator of the classical and lectin pathways. Deficiency of C1-INH causes hereditary angioedema, characterized by episodes of swelling, and can sometimes be associated with C1q nephropathy (a classical pathway-mediated glomerular disease), which typically presents with low C3 and C4. This does not fit the clinical picture (MPGN Type II) or the lab findings (normal C4).
- (C) **C5 convertase:** C5 convertase (C3bBbC3b) is the enzyme responsible for cleaving C5 into C5a and C5b. While C5 convertase activity is increased in DDD, it is not the *deficient* target. The problem lies in the *regulation* of the upstream C3 convertase (C3bBb), leading to excessive C5 convertase formation. C5 convertase itself is not deficient; it's overactive due to lack of control.
- (D) **Membrane attack complex (MAC):** The MAC (C5b-9) is the terminal effector complex of the complement system. While complement activation leads to MAC formation, MAC deficiency typically causes increased susceptibility to Neisseria infections and is not directly associated with MPGN Type II. Furthermore, MAC is a downstream product, not the primary deficient regulatory target in this disease.
- (E) **Factor B:** Factor B is a component of the C3 convertase (C3bBb). While mutations in Factor B can rarely cause complement dysregulation, deficiencies are usually associated with increased susceptibility to bacterial infections. Autoantibodies against Factor B are extremely rare. Factor H deficiency is a much more common cause of DDD.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | MPGN Type II (DDD)                                  | C1 Inhibitor Deficiency (HAE/C1q Nephropathy) | Factor B Deficiency                     |
|----------------------|------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------|------------------------------------------|
| **Complement Pathway** | Alternative Pathway                                  | Classical/Lectin Pathway                      | Alternative Pathway                      |
| **Serum C3**         | Low                                                  | Low                                         | Normal/High (Increased susceptibility to infection) |
| **Serum C4**         | Normal                                               | Low                                         | Normal                                     |
| **Biopsy (EM)**      | Dense deposits ("tram-track")                        | Subepithelial deposits (C1q nephropathy)     | Usually normal                           |
| **Key Defect**       | Dysregulation of Alternative Pathway (e.g., Factor H) | Deficiency/Dysfunction of C1-INH            | Deficiency/Dysfunction of Factor B        |
| **Clinical**         | Nephritic/Nephrotic syndrome, low C3                 | Angioedema, C1q nephropathy                 | Increased infection risk                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
MPGN Type II (Dense Deposit Disease) is characterized by low serum C3, normal C4, and "tram-track" deposits on electron microscopy, resulting from uncontrolled alternative complement pathway activation. The most common underlying defect involves complement regulatory proteins, particularly Factor H. Differentiate this from C3 glomerulopathy (which also has low C3 and tram-track deposits but lacks the specific immune deposits seen in MPGN Type I/II) and other causes of glomerulonephritis with different complement patterns (e.g., SLE nephritis with low C3/C4, post-infectious GN with low C3).

<a id='question-062'></a>

### Question 062: Renal Pathology: Systemic Disease (Secondary)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the renal manifestations and underlying pathophysiology of systemic diseases, specifically amyloidosis, including the types of amyloid deposits, their staining characteristics, and associated clinical features. (理解全身性疾病的肾脏表现及其潜在的病理生理机制，特别是淀粉样变性，包括淀粉样蛋白的类型、染色特征和相关的临床特征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a 15-year history of rheumatoid arthritis (RA), treated chronically with methotrexate and etanercept, presents to the emergency department with worsening lower extremity edema, fatigue, and proteinuria detected on routine urinalysis. His blood pressure is 145/90 mmHg, and serum creatinine is elevated at 2.5 mg/dL (normal range: 0.6-1.2 mg/dL). Urinalysis shows 4+ protein. A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy reveals glomeruli with amorphous, eosinophilic deposits in the mesangium and capillary loops, causing segmental sclerosis. Congo red staining shows apple-green birefringence under polarized light. Immunohistochemistry is positive for lambda light chains.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism leading to the renal pathology in this patient?

(A) Deposition of immune complexes containing circulating antigens, leading to complement activation and glomerular injury.
(B) Accumulation of misfolded proteins forming fibrils that deposit extracellularly, disrupting normal tissue architecture and function.
(C) Formation of granulomas within the glomeruli and interstitium due to chronic inflammation, causing obstruction and fibrosis.
(D) Direct cytotoxic effects of circulating antibodies against glomerular basement membrane components, leading to linear IgG deposition.
(E) Deposition of calcium phosphate crystals within the renal tubules and interstitium, leading to tubular obstruction and inflammation.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with long-standing rheumatoid arthritis (RA). RA is a chronic inflammatory autoimmune disease.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Worsening edema, fatigue, proteinuria, elevated creatinine (renal failure). These point towards significant kidney disease, likely nephrotic syndrome or nephritic syndrome with renal insufficiency.
*   **Diagnostic Findings:**
    *   **Proteinuria (4+):** Indicates significant glomerular damage.
    *   **Elevated Creatinine:** Confirms impaired kidney function.
    *   **Renal Biopsy (Light Microscopy):** Amorphous, eosinophilic deposits in glomeruli (mesangium and capillary loops), causing segmental sclerosis. This morphology is characteristic of amyloidosis or other types of immune complex deposition.
    *   **Congo Red Staining:** Apple-green birefringence under polarized light. This is the pathognomonic finding for amyloid deposits.
    *   **Immunohistochemistry:** Positive for lambda light chains. This indicates the amyloid is composed of immunoglobulin light chains (AL amyloidosis), often associated with plasma cell dyscrasias or chronic inflammatory conditions like RA.
*   **Connecting Clues:** The patient has chronic RA, a known risk factor for secondary (AA) amyloidosis, although AL amyloidosis can also occur. The biopsy findings (eosinophilic deposits, Congo red positivity with apple-green birefringence, lambda light chain positivity) definitively point to amyloidosis. The question asks for the underlying mechanism.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Amyloidosis:** A group of diseases characterized by the extracellular deposition of insoluble amyloid fibrils in various tissues and organs.
*   **Amyloid Fibril Formation:** Amyloid fibrils are formed from misfolded proteins that aggregate into beta-pleated sheets, which then assemble into non-branching fibrils. These fibrils have a characteristic cross-beta structure.
*   **Types of Amyloidosis:**
    *   **AL Amyloidosis (Primary):** Caused by deposition of immunoglobulin light chains (kappa or lambda) produced by a clonal population of plasma cells (e.g., multiple myeloma).
    *   **AA Amyloidosis (Secondary):** Caused by deposition of serum amyloid A (SAA), an acute-phase reactant protein, in response to chronic inflammatory conditions (e.g., RA, inflammatory bowel disease, chronic infections).
    *   **ATTR Amyloidosis (Hereditary or Wild-Type):** Caused by deposition of transthyretin (TTR).
*   **Mechanism in this Case:** The patient has chronic RA and the biopsy shows amyloid deposits positive for lambda light chains. While AA amyloidosis is classically associated with RA (SAA protein), AL amyloidosis can also occur, especially in patients with long-standing inflammation. The lambda light chain positivity strongly suggests AL amyloidosis, where misfolded lambda light chains form fibrils. Regardless of the specific protein (SAA or light chain), the fundamental mechanism involves the misfolding and aggregation of proteins into amyloid fibrils that deposit extracellularly. These deposits disrupt tissue structure and function, leading to organ damage (in this case, kidney damage manifesting as proteinuria and renal failure). The core mechanism is the accumulation of misfolded proteins forming fibrils.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Deposition of immune complexes containing circulating antigens, leading to complement activation and glomerular injury.** This describes immune complex-mediated glomerulonephritis (e.g., lupus nephritis, post-infectious glomerulonephritis, IgA nephropathy). While RA can be associated with immune complex deposition, the characteristic Congo red positivity and apple-green birefringence are specific to amyloid, not typical immune complexes. The deposits in amyloidosis are not primarily composed of circulating antigens complexed with antibodies in the same way as in typical immune complex GN.
*   **(C) Formation of granulomas within the glomeruli and interstitium due to chronic inflammation, causing obstruction and fibrosis.** This describes granulomatous interstitial nephritis or granulomatous glomerulonephritis, often seen in conditions like sarcoidosis, tuberculosis, or certain infections. The biopsy findings (amorphous eosinophilic deposits, Congo red positivity) are not consistent with granuloma formation.
*   **(D) Direct cytotoxic effects of circulating antibodies against glomerular basement membrane components, leading to linear IgG deposition.** This describes anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) disease (e.g., Goodpasture syndrome). Immunofluorescence would show linear IgG deposition along the GBM, which is not described here. The light microscopy findings and Congo red stain are also inconsistent with anti-GBM disease.
*   **(E) Deposition of calcium phosphate crystals within the renal tubules and interstitium, leading to tubular obstruction and inflammation.** This describes crystal-induced nephropathy (e.g., calcium oxalate deposition in myeloma cast nephropathy, or uric acid deposition in gouty nephropathy). While patients with chronic kidney disease can develop crystal deposition, the characteristic findings here (glomerular deposits, Congo red positivity) point specifically to amyloidosis, not crystal deposition.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Amyloidosis (AL/AA)                                  | Immune Complex GN (e.g., Lupus Nephritis) | Granulomatous Nephritis (e.g., Sarcoidosis) | Anti-GBM Disease (Goodpasture) | Crystal Nephropathy (e.g., Myeloma Cast) |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :----------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Pathogenesis** | Misfolded protein aggregation into amyloid fibrils     | Immune complex deposition                | Granuloma formation                          | Anti-GBM antibody attack      | Crystal deposition                       |
| **Light Microscopy**     | Amorphous eosinophilic deposits, segmental sclerosis | Variable (diffuse, focal, membranous, etc.) | Granulomas in interstitium/glomeruli         | Crescent formation, GBM damage | Tubular casts, interstitial inflammation |
| **Congo Red Stain**      | Positive (+), Apple-green birefringence (polarized) | Negative (-)                             | Negative (-)                                 | Negative (-)                   | Negative (-)                             |
| **Immunofluorescence**  | Variable (e.g., lambda/kappa light chains)           | Granular deposits (IgG, IgM, IgA, C3)     | Variable, may show immune complexes         | Linear IgG along GBM          | Variable, may show light chains          |
| **Associated Conditions** | Chronic inflammation (AA), Plasma cell dyscrasia (AL) | Autoimmune diseases (SLE), Infections    | Sarcoidosis, Infections, Granulomatosis      | Autoimmune (Goodpasture)      | Myeloma, Gout, Hypercalcemia             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Amyloidosis is characterized by the deposition of misfolded proteins forming amyloid fibrils, confirmed by Congo red staining showing apple-green birefringence. Renal involvement is common, presenting with proteinuria and renal failure. Distinguish amyloidosis from other causes of glomerular disease like immune complex GN (granular IF), anti-GBM disease (linear IF), granulomatous nephritis (granulomas on histology), and crystal nephropathy (crystals on histology). The key diagnostic feature for amyloidosis is the Congo red stain.

<a id='question-063'></a>

### Question 063: Renal Pathology: Focus 02-13 (Glomerular disease may present clinically as nephr)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology of IgA nephropathy, specifically the role of galactose-alpha-1,3-galactose (α-gal) epitope in IgA1 glycoform modification, its deposition in the glomeruli, and the resulting immune complex-mediated glomerular injury. (IgA肾病的发病机制理解，特别是IgA1糖蛋白中半乳糖基-α-1,3-半乳糖基 (α-gal) 表位的修饰、在肾小球中的沉积以及由此引起的免疫复合物介导的肾小球损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with gross hematuria following an upper respiratory tract infection. He reports experiencing similar episodes of painless hematuria, often coinciding with infections, since his early twenties. His past medical history is unremarkable except for occasional seasonal allergies. He denies fever, flank pain, dysuria, or recent trauma. Physical examination reveals mild pallor but is otherwise normal. Urinalysis shows macroscopic hematuria with dysmorphic red blood cells and mild proteinuria (1+). Serum creatinine is 1.2 mg/dL (reference range 0.7-1.3 mg/dL), and serum albumin is 4.0 g/dL (reference range 3.5-5.0 g/dL). A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows diffuse mesangial proliferation and hypercellularity. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals granular deposits of IgA, C3, and C1q in the mesangium. Electron microscopy shows electron-dense deposits within the mesangial matrix. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *FGFA5* gene, known to be associated with increased susceptibility to IgA nephropathy. The patient is started on an ACE inhibitor and advised to avoid strenuous exercise. Which of the following molecular modifications is most directly implicated in the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) Aberrant glycosylation of IgA1 heavy chains leading to exposure of the galactose-alpha-1,3-galactose (α-gal) epitope.
(B) Defective synthesis of the glomerular basement membrane (GBM) collagen IV alpha-3 chain, leading to GBM thinning and rupture.
(C) Formation of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies targeting the non-collagenous domain (NC1) of the alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen.
(D) Deposition of immune complexes containing rheumatoid factor (RF) and cryoglobulins within the glomerular capillaries.
(E) Excessive production of C5a leading to neutrophil recruitment and glomerular damage via complement activation.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male with recurrent gross hematuria following URI, suggesting IgA nephropathy (Berger's disease).
- **Clinical Presentation:** Recurrent macroscopic hematuria, often triggered by infections (synpharyngitic hematuria), is classic for IgA nephropathy.
- **Lab Findings:** Hematuria (dysmorphic RBCs), mild proteinuria, normal renal function (Cr 1.2 mg/dL), normal albumin. These findings are consistent with early or mild IgA nephropathy.
- **Renal Biopsy:**
    - **Light Microscopy:** Mesangial proliferation and hypercellularity are characteristic histological findings in IgA nephropathy.
    - **Immunofluorescence:** Granular mesangial deposits of IgA are the hallmark diagnostic feature. Co-deposition of C3 and C1q is common.
    - **Electron Microscopy:** Electron-dense deposits in the mesangium confirm immune complex deposition.
- **Genetic Factor:** Heterozygous mutation in *FGFA5* is associated with increased susceptibility to IgA nephropathy, suggesting a genetic predisposition.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most direct* molecular modification implicated in the pathogenesis.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **IgA Nephropathy Pathogenesis:** The core defect in IgA nephropathy involves the abnormal glycosylation of the IgA1 heavy chain. Specifically, the hinge region of IgA1 is normally glycosylated with galactose and N-acetylgalactosamine. In IgA nephropathy, there is a deficiency in O-linked glycosylation, specifically the addition of sialic acid and galactose to the N-acetylgalactosamine residue. This deficiency leads to the exposure of the galactose-alpha-1,3-galactose (α-gal) epitope on the IgA1 molecule.
- **α-gal Epitope:** This exposed α-gal epitope is normally found on non-human galactose residues. In humans, it's usually masked by sialylation. The exposure of this epitope is thought to make the IgA1 molecule neoantigenic.
- **Immune Complex Formation:** These modified IgA1 molecules form immune complexes in the circulation or locally within the glomeruli.
- **Glomerular Deposition:** These immune complexes deposit predominantly in the mesangium.
- **Inflammation & Injury:** The deposited IgA1-containing immune complexes activate the complement system (alternative pathway) and recruit inflammatory cells (mesangial cells, macrophages), leading to mesangial proliferation, matrix expansion, and ultimately glomerular damage, proteinuria, and hematuria.
- **Molecular Target:** The key molecular modification is the aberrant glycosylation exposing the α-gal epitope on IgA1.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Aberrant glycosylation of IgA1 heavy chains leading to exposure of the galactose-alpha-1,3-galactose (α-gal) epitope.** This is the correct answer. The vignette describes classic IgA nephropathy, and the underlying molecular defect is the abnormal glycosylation of IgA1, exposing the α-gal epitope, which is crucial for its pathogenesis.
- (B): **Defective synthesis of the glomerular basement membrane (GBM) collagen IV alpha-3 chain, leading to GBM thinning and rupture.** This describes Alport syndrome, an inherited disorder affecting type IV collagen, leading to GBM abnormalities, hematuria, proteinuria, and progressive renal failure. It is characterized by mutations in *COL4A3*, *COL4A4*, or *COL4A5* genes, not *FGFA5*, and the biopsy findings (IgA deposition) are inconsistent with Alport syndrome.
- (C): **Formation of anti-glomerular basement membrane (anti-GBM) antibodies targeting the non-collagenous domain (NC1) of the alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen.** This describes anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome), characterized by linear IgG deposition along the GBM on immunofluorescence. The clinical presentation can include rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and pulmonary hemorrhage. The biopsy findings (mesangial IgA deposits) are inconsistent with anti-GBM disease.
- (D): **Deposition of immune complexes containing rheumatoid factor (RF) and cryoglobulins within the glomerular capillaries.** This describes cryoglobulinemic glomerulonephritis, often associated with chronic infections (like HCV) or autoimmune diseases. It typically involves deposition of cryoglobulins (often IgM RF complexes) in small vessels, leading to membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) pattern. The biopsy findings (mesangial IgA deposits) are inconsistent with cryoglobulinemic GN.
- (E): **Excessive production of C5a leading to neutrophil recruitment and glomerular damage via complement activation.** While complement activation (especially the alternative pathway) plays a role in the inflammatory process in IgA nephropathy, excessive C5a production is not the primary initiating molecular defect. The primary defect is the abnormal IgA1 glycosylation leading to immune complex formation and deposition, which *then* triggers complement activation. This option describes a downstream consequence rather than the initiating molecular modification.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | IgA Nephropathy (Correct Answer) | Alport Syndrome | Anti-GBM Disease | Cryoglobulinemic GN |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :-------------- | :--------------- | :------------------ |
| **Primary Defect**      | Aberrant IgA1 glycosylation (α-gal) | Type IV Collagen | Anti-GBM Abs    | Cryoglobulin Dep.  |
| **Immune Deposit**      | IgA (mesangial)                   | None specific   | IgG (linear GBM) | Cryoglobulins (MPGN) |
| **Light Microscopy**    | Mesangial proliferation           | GBM splitting/thinning | Crescent formation | MPGN pattern       |
| **Genetic Association** | *FGFA5* (susceptibility)          | *COL4A3/4/5*    | None specific   | None specific      |
| **Clinical Trigger**    | URI (common)                      | None specific   | None specific   | Chronic infection   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The hallmark of IgA nephropathy is the deposition of IgA-containing immune complexes in the mesangium, often triggered by infections. The underlying molecular defect involves aberrant glycosylation of IgA1 heavy chains, exposing the α-gal epitope. Differentiating IgA nephropathy from other glomerulonephritides relies on recognizing the characteristic IgA deposition pattern on immunofluorescence and understanding the specific molecular defects and immune deposits associated with conditions like Alport syndrome (Type IV collagen defects), anti-GBM disease (linear IgG deposition), and cryoglobulinemic GN (cryoglobulin deposition, often MPGN pattern).

<a id='question-064'></a>

### Question 064: Renal Pathology: Focus 02-14 (Their clinical features are different.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating between acute tubular necrosis (ATN) caused by ischemia and nephrotoxic injury based on histological features and clinical context. (区分缺血性ATN和毒性ATN的组织学特征和临床背景。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a history of hypertension, type 2 diabetes mellitus, and chronic kidney disease (CKD stage 3) is admitted to the intensive care unit following a large anterior wall myocardial infarction complicated by cardiogenic shock. During his hospitalization, he develops anuria and acute kidney injury (AKI). His creatinine rises from a baseline of 1.8 mg/dL to 5.5 mg/dL within 48 hours. Urinalysis shows muddy brown casts. Renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy reveals tubular epithelial cell necrosis, detachment, and sloughing into the tubular lumen, forming casts. The proximal tubules show flattened epithelial cells with loss of brush border. Distal tubules show vacuolization. The interstitium shows mild edema and scattered inflammatory cells. There is no evidence of glomerular injury or significant interstitial fibrosis beyond that expected for his baseline CKD. Immunohistochemical staining for KIM-1 (Kidney Injury Molecule-1) is strongly positive in the proximal tubules.

Which of the following histological findings is MOST characteristic of the likely cause of this patient's AKI?

(A) Prominent eosinophilic thrombi within the afferent arterioles and glomerular capillaries.
(B) Widespread necrosis and sloughing of tubular epithelial cells, particularly in the proximal tubules and thick ascending limb, with minimal interstitial inflammation.
(C) Diffuse granular deposits in the glomerular basement membrane and mesangium, along with crescent formation in Bowman's space.
(D) Segmental sclerosis and hyalinosis of the glomerular capillaries, often accompanied by podocyte effacement.
(E) Necrotizing granulomas within the renal interstitium, often associated with vasculitis affecting small vessels.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with comorbidities (HTN, DM2, CKD stage 3) increasing risk for AKI.
- **Clinical Event:** Large anterior MI complicated by cardiogenic shock. This strongly suggests a period of systemic hypoperfusion and potential renal ischemia.
- **AKI Presentation:** Anuria, rapid rise in creatinine (1.8 to 5.5 mg/dL in 48 hours), indicating severe AKI.
- **Urinalysis:** Muddy brown casts are characteristic of acute tubular necrosis (ATN).
- **Renal Biopsy (Light Microscopy):**
    - Tubular epithelial cell necrosis, detachment, sloughing into lumen (forming casts) - hallmark of ATN.
    - Flattened proximal tubules with loss of brush border - indicates proximal tubular injury.
    - Distal tubule vacuolization - can be seen in both ischemic and toxic ATN.
    - No glomerular injury - rules out primary glomerular diseases.
    - Mild interstitial edema/inflammation - typical for ATN, not severe interstitial nephritis.
    - Baseline CKD fibrosis - expected given history.
- **Immunohistochemistry:** Strong KIM-1 positivity in proximal tubules. KIM-1 is an early marker of proximal tubular injury, upregulated in ATN, particularly ischemic ATN.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST characteristic histological finding of the *likely cause* of AKI. Given the clinical context (cardiogenic shock leading to hypoperfusion) and the presence of muddy brown casts and proximal tubular injury (flattened cells, loss of brush border, KIM-1 positivity), ischemic ATN is the most probable diagnosis.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Ischemic ATN:** Occurs due to prolonged renal hypoperfusion (e.g., shock, severe dehydration). The most vulnerable cells are those with high metabolic demands and exposure to toxins: proximal tubule cells (especially S3 segment) and thick ascending limb cells.
    - **Mechanism:** Hypoxia leads to ATP depletion, failure of ion pumps (Na+/K+-ATPase), cell swelling, detachment from basement membrane, and eventual necrosis. Sloughed cells form casts obstructing tubules.
    - **Histology:** Characterized by widespread necrosis and sloughing of tubular epithelial cells, particularly in the proximal tubules (S1, S2, S3 segments) and thick ascending limb. The distal convoluted tubule is relatively spared. Interstitial inflammation is usually mild initially.
    - **KIM-1:** Kidney Injury Molecule-1 is a type I transmembrane protein highly expressed in proximal tubule epithelial cells. Its expression is markedly upregulated in response to ischemic or toxic injury, making it a sensitive marker for ATN, especially involving proximal tubules.
- **Toxic ATN:** Caused by nephrotoxins (e.g., aminoglycosides, contrast dye, cisplatin, ethylene glycol). Proximal tubule cells are also the primary target due to their role in reabsorption and secretion.
    - **Mechanism:** Toxins directly damage tubular cells, leading to necrosis, apoptosis, or dysfunction.
    - **Histology:** Similar to ischemic ATN (tubular necrosis, sloughing, cast formation), but often more patchy or diffuse depending on the toxin. Distal tubule injury can be more prominent with certain toxins (e.g., cisplatin). Interstitial inflammation may be more pronounced.
- **Distinguishing Ischemic vs. Toxic ATN:** Histologically, they can be very similar, especially in the early stages. However, ischemic ATN often shows more prominent injury to the proximal tubules (S3 segment) and thick ascending limb, while toxic ATN might show more distal tubule involvement or specific patterns depending on the toxin. Clinically, the history is crucial. In this case, the history of cardiogenic shock strongly points towards ischemia. The strong KIM-1 positivity in proximal tubules also supports significant proximal tubular injury, common in ischemic ATN.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Prominent eosinophilic thrombi within the afferent arterioles and glomerular capillaries.** This describes thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA), such as hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS) or thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura (TTP), or malignant hypertension. These conditions primarily affect the microvasculature of the glomeruli and arterioles, leading to glomerular ischemia and damage, not primarily tubular necrosis as seen here. The clinical presentation (cardiogenic shock) does not fit TMA.
- (C): **Diffuse granular deposits in the glomerular basement membrane and mesangium, along with crescent formation in Bowman's space.** This describes rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis (RPGN), often associated with anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture's), immune complex deposition (post-infectious GN, lupus nephritis), or pauci-immune GN (ANCA-associated vasculitis). The biopsy findings clearly show tubular injury, not primary glomerular disease with crescents.
- (D): **Segmental sclerosis and hyalinosis of the glomerular capillaries, often accompanied by podocyte effacement.** This describes focal segmental glomerulosclerosis (FSGS), a common cause of nephrotic syndrome. While FSGS can lead to CKD and potentially AKI in certain contexts, the primary lesion is glomerular, not tubular necrosis. The biopsy findings are inconsistent with FSGS.
- (E): **Necrotizing granulomas within the renal interstitium, often associated with vasculitis affecting small vessels.** This describes granulomatous interstitial nephritis, often seen in conditions like sarcoidosis, tuberculosis, or certain drug reactions. While interstitial inflammation is present, it is mild, and there are no granulomas described. The primary lesion is tubular necrosis, not interstitial granulomas.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Ischemic ATN (Correct Answer)                                  | Toxic ATN                                                | TMA (A)                                         | RPGN (C)                                        | FSGS (D)                                        | Granulomatous IN (E)                            |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Lesion**      | Tubular necrosis & sloughing (esp. proximal tubule, TAL)        | Tubular necrosis & sloughing (esp. proximal tubule)        | Glomerular/Arteriolar Thrombosis                 | Glomerular Inflammation (Crescents)              | Glomerular Sclerosis                             | Interstitial Granulomas                         |
| **Clinical Context**    | Hypoperfusion (shock, dehydration)                             | Exposure to nephrotoxins (drugs, contrast)                | Thrombocytopenia, Microangiopathic Hemolysis    | Rapidly declining renal function, Hematuria     | Nephrotic Syndrome                               | Systemic disease (Sarcoid, TB), Drug reaction   |
| **Urinalysis**          | Muddy brown casts                                               | Muddy brown casts                                          | Hematuria, Proteinuria                            | Hematuria, Proteinuria, RBC casts                | Proteinuria                                      | Sterile pyuria, Eosinophiluria (sometimes)      |
| **Histology (Tubules)** | Widespread necrosis, sloughing, cast formation, flattened cells | Widespread necrosis, sloughing, cast formation            | Minimal tubular changes unless severe ischemia   | Minimal tubular changes unless severe ischemia   | Minimal tubular changes unless severe ischemia   | Variable tubular injury, Granulomas in interstitium |
| **Histology (Glomeruli)** | Usually normal                                                 | Usually normal                                             | Thrombotic microangiopathy                      | Crescents, Inflammation                          | Segmental Sclerosis, Hyalinosis                  | Usually normal                                  |
| **Histology (Interstitium)** | Mild edema, inflammation                                      | Variable, can be more prominent than ischemic ATN          | Minimal unless severe ischemia                   | Minimal unless severe ischemia                   | Minimal unless severe ischemia                   | Granulomas, Inflammation                        |
| **KIM-1**               | Strongly positive (esp. proximal tubules)                       | Positive (esp. proximal tubules)                          | Not characteristic                               | Not characteristic                               | Not characteristic                               | Not characteristic                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute tubular necrosis (ATN) is the most common cause of intrinsic AKI. It is characterized by tubular epithelial cell injury and necrosis, leading to cast formation and obstruction. Ischemic ATN (due to hypoperfusion) and toxic ATN (due to nephrotoxins) are the two main types, often indistinguishable histologically but differentiated by clinical context. Key histological features include tubular necrosis, sloughing, and cast formation, particularly affecting the proximal tubules and thick ascending limb. KIM-1 is a sensitive marker for proximal tubular injury in ATN. Differentiating ATN from other causes of AKI like glomerulonephritis (crescents, glomerular lesions), thrombotic microangiopathy (thrombi), or interstitial nephritis (granulomas, prominent interstitial inflammation) is crucial.

<a id='question-065'></a>

### Question 065: Renal Pathology: Focus 02-15 (Table 15 1. Clinical Syndromes in Glomerular Disea)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology and histological features of membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN), specifically Type II (Dense Deposit Disease), and differentiating it from other glomerular diseases based on clinical presentation and diagnostic findings. (理解膜性增生性肾小球肾炎（MPGN）的病理生理学和组织学特征，特别是II型（致密沉积病），并根据临床表现和诊断结果与其他肾小球疾病区分。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with complaints of worsening fatigue, lower extremity edema, and dark-colored urine over the past week. His past medical history is significant for recurrent upper respiratory infections since childhood and a recent diagnosis of hepatitis C infection (HCV genotype 3) confirmed 3 months ago. He denies any history of recent streptococcal infection, autoimmune disease, or intravenous drug use. On examination, his blood pressure is 160/100 mmHg, and he has 3+ pitting edema bilaterally. Urinalysis reveals 3+ proteinuria, hematuria with dysmorphic red blood cells, and granular casts. Serum creatinine is elevated at 2.5 mg/dL (reference range 0.6-1.2 mg/dL), and C3 levels are markedly decreased (45 mg/dL, reference range 85-190 mg/dL), while C4 levels are normal (120 mg/dL, reference range 100-400 mg/dL). A renal biopsy is performed. Light microscopy shows hypercellular glomeruli with mesangial expansion and endocapillary proliferation. Immunofluorescence microscopy reveals diffuse granular deposition of IgG and C3 along the glomerular capillary walls and in the mesangium. Electron microscopy demonstrates characteristic subendothelial and mesangial electron-dense deposits, forming "tram-track" appearances along the glomerular basement membrane (GBM). Which of the following is the most likely underlying pathophysiological mechanism leading to this patient's renal disease?

(A) Immune complex deposition mediated by anti-GBM antibodies, leading to linear IgG staining on immunofluorescence.
(B) Complement activation via the alternative pathway due to C3 nephritic factor (C3NeF) autoantibodies, resulting in C3 convertase stabilization.
(C) Formation of subepithelial immune deposits containing IgG and C3, characteristic of post-infectious glomerulonephritis.
(D) Deposition of circulating immune complexes containing hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg) and antibodies, leading to a "comet tail" appearance on immunofluorescence.
(E) Activation of the classical complement pathway by anti-dsDNA antibodies, leading to decreased C3 and C4 levels and "wire loop" lesions on electron microscopy.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male with worsening fatigue, edema, dark urine (suggesting hematuria/proteinuria), hypertension, and elevated creatinine (acute kidney injury).
*   **Past Medical History:** Recurrent infections (possible immune dysregulation), recent HCV diagnosis (can be associated with MPGN, but less commonly Type II).
*   **Urinalysis:** 3+ proteinuria, hematuria (dysmorphic RBCs suggest glomerular origin), granular casts (suggest tubular damage/glomerular disease).
*   **Labs:** Elevated creatinine (AKI), markedly decreased C3, normal C4 (suggests alternative pathway complement activation).
*   **Biopsy Findings:**
    *   Light Microscopy: Hypercellular glomeruli, mesangial expansion, endocapillary proliferation (consistent with MPGN).
    *   Immunofluorescence: Diffuse granular IgG and C3 deposition (consistent with immune complex deposition).
    *   Electron Microscopy: Subendothelial and mesangial electron-dense deposits, "tram-track" appearance (hallmark of MPGN, especially Type II/DDD).
*   **Synthesis:** The clinical presentation (nephritic syndrome with AKI, hypertension, edema, hematuria, proteinuria), laboratory findings (low C3, normal C4), and biopsy results (MPGN pattern with tram-tracks) strongly point towards Membranoproliferative Glomerulonephritis (MPGN). The low C3 and normal C4 specifically suggest activation of the alternative complement pathway. MPGN Type II, also known as Dense Deposit Disease (DDD), is characterized by these features and is often associated with autoantibodies against C3 convertase (C3NeF).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **MPGN Type II (DDD):** This is a primary glomerulonephritis characterized by the deposition of dense, electron-dense material within the glomerular basement membrane and mesangium.
*   **Pathophysiology:** The underlying mechanism involves dysregulation of the alternative complement pathway. In most cases (~80%), patients have autoantibodies directed against C3 convertase (C3bBb), known as C3 nephritic factor (C3NeF). These autoantibodies stabilize the C3 convertase, leading to its continuous activation and excessive cleavage of C3 into C3a and C3b. This results in sustained complement activation, deposition of C3b fragments, formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC), and glomerular injury.
*   **Complement Pathways:**
    *   **Classical Pathway:** Activated by antigen-antibody complexes (IgG, IgM). Leads to consumption of both C3 and C4.
    *   **Alternative Pathway:** Activated spontaneously on cell surfaces or by certain microbial products. Normally tightly regulated. Dysregulation (e.g., by C3NeF) leads to excessive C3 activation with minimal C4 consumption.
*   **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's low C3 and normal C4 levels are classic for alternative pathway activation. The biopsy findings (tram-tracks) are characteristic of DDD/MPGN Type II.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Anti-GBM disease (Goodpasture syndrome):** This involves antibodies against the alpha-3 chain of type IV collagen in the GBM. It typically presents with rapidly progressive glomerulonephritis and pulmonary hemorrhage. Immunofluorescence shows *linear* IgG deposition along the GBM, not granular. C3 levels are usually normal. This is incorrect because the vignette describes *granular* deposition and low C3.
- (C): **Post-infectious glomerulonephritis (PIGN):** Typically occurs after streptococcal infection (though other infections can cause it). Presents with nephritic syndrome. Immunofluorescence shows granular deposits of IgG and C3 ("starry sky" pattern). Electron microscopy shows subepithelial "humps". C3 is usually low, but C4 may also be low (classical pathway activation). This is incorrect because the biopsy shows subendothelial/mesangial deposits and tram-tracks, not subepithelial humps, and the clinical context (recent HCV, recurrent infections) is less typical for classic PIGN.
- (D): **Hepatitis B-associated glomerulonephritis:** Can cause membranous nephropathy or MPGN. Immune complexes containing HBsAg deposit in the glomeruli. Immunofluorescence shows granular deposits of IgG, IgM, C3, and often HBsAg ("comet tail" appearance). C3 and C4 may be low. This is incorrect because the patient has HCV, not HBV, and the characteristic "comet tail" appearance is not mentioned. While HCV can be associated with MPGN (often Type I), the low C3/normal C4 pattern points more strongly to Type II.
- (E): **Lupus nephritis (Class IV):** Systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) can cause various forms of glomerulonephritis, including diffuse proliferative lupus nephritis (Class IV). This involves immune complex deposition (IgG, IgM, C3) and complement activation (both classical and alternative). C3 and C4 levels are typically *low* due to classical pathway activation by anti-dsDNA antibodies. Electron microscopy shows subendothelial and mesangial deposits, sometimes with "wire loop" lesions (subendothelial deposits thickening the GBM). This is incorrect because the vignette describes *normal* C4 levels, which argues against significant classical pathway activation typical of SLE. Also, the specific "tram-track" appearance is more characteristic of DDD/MPGN Type II.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | MPGN Type II (DDD) | Anti-GBM Disease | PIGN                 | HBV-GN               | Lupus Nephritis (Class IV) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------- | :--------------- | :------------------- | :------------------- | :------------------------- |
| **Clinical**            | Nephritic/Nephrotic | RPGN, Lung Hemorrhage | Nephritic (post-infxn) | Variable             | Variable (often systemic)  |
| **C3 Level**            | Low                | Normal           | Low                  | Low                  | Low                       |
| **C4 Level**            | Normal             | Normal           | Normal/Low            | Normal/Low            | Low                        |
| **IF Pattern**          | Granular IgG, C3   | Linear IgG       | Granular IgG, C3      | Granular IgG, C3, HBsAg | Granular IgG, IgM, C3      |
| **EM Deposits**         | Dense, Tram-tracks | None             | Subepithelial Humps  | Sub/Epi deposits     | Subendothelial, Mesangial  |
| **Pathophysiology**     | Alt. Pathway (C3NeF) | Anti-GBM Ab     | Immune Complexes     | Immune Complexes     | Immune Complexes           |
| **Key Association**     | C3NeF              | Goodpasture's   | Strep Infection      | HBV Infection        | SLE                        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN) Type II (Dense Deposit Disease) is characterized by low C3 and normal C4 levels due to alternative pathway complement activation, often mediated by C3 nephritic factor (C3NeF). Electron microscopy shows characteristic dense deposits and "tram-track" GBM splitting. Key differentials include other forms of MPGN (Type I, associated with classical pathway activation and low C3/C4), post-infectious GN (subepithelial humps), anti-GBM disease (linear IF), and lupus nephritis (low C3/C4, often systemic symptoms).
